I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind has been working differently since your sister passed away compared to how it was before?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. That’s really why I came in, because my mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to.\n\nI’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—nothing complicated, something I could’ve done in my sleep before—and the numbers and words just… scatter on me. Like I know what I meant to put down, and then it’s gone, or it’s all mixed up. I’ll stare at the paper, and then I start thinking about where I put the pencil sharpener, or whether the hinges on that old cabinet ever got fixed, and then I’ve lost the whole thread.\n\nSo compared to before… yes. It feels different. It feels like I can’t keep hold of my own thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you needed assistance with daily tasks that you used to be able to do on your own since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. More than I like to admit.\n\nI used to handle things at church without really thinking—setting things up, keeping track of little responsibilities. And the garden, that was mine. I knew where everything was supposed to go, even if the tomatoes always got too ambitious and took over the row. My sister used to laugh about that, actually… said I planted like I was trying to feed the whole county.\n\nBut lately those things have just sort of… fallen away. I haven’t kept up with the church responsibilities, or the garden, or the youth maker program the way I used to.\n\nMy wife has had to step in some, and the pastor too. Especially with the workshop. I don’t really feel safe being out there by myself now, with the tools and all that. I hate saying that. I used to be the one showing the kids how to use things properly. Now I need someone else making sure I’m all right.\n\nDoctor: Do you require help with personal care, such as bathing or dressing, that you used to be able to do by yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d say that, no. Bathing and dressing… I mean, I still get myself together.\n\nSometimes I stand there longer than I should, trying to remember why I opened the drawer, and then I’ll notice an old shirt I wore to one of the church picnics—there was this one summer it rained so hard the potato salad floated, or nearly did—and then I’m thinking about that instead of what I was doing.\n\nBut needing someone to bathe me or dress me… no, I don’t think so. Not like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how your friends and family interact with you since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… they have, I think. My wife watches me more closely now. Not in a cruel way—she’s a good woman—but I can tell she’s waiting to see if I’ll forget something or get myself into trouble.\n\nAnd the pastor, too. He’s had to step in with things I used to handle at church. I let those responsibilities slip, and the garden’s gone mostly wild, and the youth maker program… well, that one hurts. I used to be the steady hand there.\n\nThe workshop especially. I don’t really feel safe being in there by myself anymore, with the tools and all. Funny, I used to tell the kids, “Respect the blade before the blade teaches you,” and they’d all roll their eyes at me… but now my wife and the pastor are the ones making sure someone’s around. So yes, people treat me more carefully now. More like I need watching.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing persistent feelings of sadness or hopelessness since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have.\n\nMost days I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling right here in my chest. It’s hard to explain. Like something’s been scooped out and left empty, but somehow it still weighs a ton.\n\nAnd even when my wife is talking kindly to me—she does that, she’ll bring me coffee and try to sound cheerful, and she’s always been better with mornings than I am—I still feel like I’m sitting at the bottom of a well. I can hear her, I know she’s there, but everything feels far away.\n\nSo… yes. Sad, hopeless… that’s been there most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your ability to think and remember has gotten worse over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. That’s part of why I came in, really. My mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to.\n\nI’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I could’ve done without even thinking before—and the words just scatter. The numbers too. I’ll have the pencil in my hand and then I’m staring at the page like it’s in another language.\n\nI used to be good with that sort of thing. Kept things straight. Now it feels like trying to carry water in my hands.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your memory and thinking problems have gotten worse since you first noticed them three weeks ago?\n\nPatient: Yes… it feels worse, or at least it feels like I can’t trust it anymore.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. My mind just won’t hold onto things. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do almost in my sleep, honestly—and then the numbers and the words just… scatter on me.\n\nI’ll be holding the pencil, and I know what I meant to write, and then it’s gone. Or it’s all jumbled up. And that program, you know, those kids used to make the funniest crooked little birdhouses… but—sorry. The point is, yes, it feels like it’s gotten harder to think straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you need help with everyday tasks that you used to manage on your own?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do now. More than I want to admit.\n\nI’ve let things slip that I used to handle without thinking—church responsibilities, my garden, the youth maker program. The garden especially, it used to be sort of my quiet place… tomatoes never listen, but at least they’re honest, you know? Anyway, I’m not keeping up with any of it.\n\nMy wife has had to step in, and our pastor too, with things I was supposed to manage. And the workshop… I don’t really feel safe being in there by myself anymore. I used to practically live out there, but now I worry I’ll make a mistake or forget what I’m doing. So yes, I need help. More than before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yes… yes, it has. I mean, I don’t like saying that, but it’s not just little forgetful moments.
